The working spelling today is a branch-selecting factory `-> Own[Cancellable[T]]` (an author-chosen erasure boundary), which also covers heterogeneous-slot needs (see 6a). (4c) *Temp-receiver auto-materialization* -- `c = Counter(40).bump(5)` is rejected (stable-lvalue rule) but COULD work for the provably-local case by hoisting the rvalue receiver into a frame local (precedent: `_lift_borrowed_rvalue_args` does exactly this for inline awaits' statement-scoped borrows); deliberately not done in v1 because the handle's open-ended borrow makes an invisible compiler temp hide escape hazards -- relax once receiver-escape tracking is real, for handles consumed in the same scope with no escape. (5) *Receiver lifetime across escapes* -- a bound method-coroutine's receiver borrow is registered until consumption, but `create_task(c)` / returning the handle only WARN (the task/caller can outlive the receiver); full tracking is the post-THIR escape-analysis milestone. (6) *Borrowing a handle* -- protocol-annotated alias, bare-`Cancellable[T]` borrow param, and ternary-over-handles are all rejected (handles are consume-only); real borrow support is post-THIR work. (6a) *Erased LOCALS have no explicit spelling* -- the `Own[Cancellable[T]]` local-annotation escape hatch was REMOVED (locals never take Own[] anywhere in TPy); erased locals now arise only from signature boundaries (factory returns / Own params). If a first-class erased-local need appears (heterogeneous slot beyond bind-to-a-new-name), the vocabulary is `Box[Cancellable[T]]` -- construction compiles today, but consuming through the Box (await / create_task via take()) is unwired; design + wire it then. (6b) *Template-frame callees bind erased* -- an async def with static-protocol / `Fn` params has a templated frame whose struct name needs call-site deduction, so its binding falls back to the erased handle (a signature-visible, documented boundary); a zero-alloc form needs deduced-frame naming at bind time. Covered by `async_bind_template_fallback`. (6c) *Cross-module bind* -- the module_qual-qualified concrete-struct naming path is covered by `async_bind_cross_module` (bare-import + from-import forms). (7) *Static coroutine-only check is launderable* -- `create_task`/`run` accept any value whose static type is `Cancellable[T]`, so a conforming non-coroutine record behind a `-> Own[Cancellable[T]]` factory passes and runs where CPython raises TypeError/ValueError; declared divergence (crash-direction under CPython), a full fix needs runtime coroutine branding.
- **Protocol conformance for built-in and generic-container types (incl. conditional conformance).** Bounds + protocols already give monomorphized per-type method dispatch -- `def f[T: P](x: T) -> str: return x.m()` resolves and recurses correctly for USER types (verified). The gap that blocks all type-directed stdlib code is conformance COVERAGE: (1) built-ins already REACH structural conformance (the builtin extends-check is a positive fast-path that falls through, not a short-circuit) and conform fine to protocols whose methods they DECLARE as stub methods (e.g. `__add__` on `Int32`); but an intrinsic dunder that exists only as a C++ free function (`tpy::__str__(int32_t)`, `dunder.hpp`), not a declared stub method, is invisible to the structural check -- so `Int32` fails a `Stringable {__str__}` bound. Leaves whose required method is C++-only can't participate. (2) No conditional/parametric conformance for generic containers -- no way to state "`list[T]` conforms to `P` when `T` conforms to `P`" (a blanket instance); conformance is per-concrete structural match only. (3) (minor) type-arg inference doesn't fire for a protocol-bounded generic from a built-in arg (needs explicit `[T]`). Fix surface: make built-ins' C++-only intrinsic dunders visible to `sema/protocols.py` structural conformance (declare them as stub methods mapped to the C++ free function, or have conformance consult the intrinsic surface), plus a conditional-conformance concept in the type system. The existing stdlib `Stringable`/`Truthy`/`Representable` protocols are the direct test bed. This is the SHARED prerequisite for two big goals: (a) type-directed serialization in pure stdlib -- zero-copy `json.dumps`/`csv`/`repr` (see the json entry under Next); and (b) **moving `print`/`str` OUT of the compiler** -- they are compiler-blessed today (`gen_print` + the C++ `printing.hpp` printers) precisely because containers/scalars can't conform to a `Display`-style protocol in stdlib. Foundational language feature -- design-doc-worthy `/tpy-add-feature`, not an ad-hoc change. Exploratory design started in `docs/PROTOCOL_CONFORMANCE_DESIGN.md` (keep structural + add nominal impls, singledispatch-shaped; open pivot is method-protocol vs typeclass-function dispatch). Surfaced designing zero-copy json.dumps (the free-function-overload route also dead-ends: `render(e: T)` inside `render[T](list[T])` can't resolve because overloads resolve once with `T` abstract, not per instantiation -- which is why the protocol/bound route, not overloads, is the path).
- **CPython-runnable spellings for the marker vocabulary (`Own`/`readonly`/`Send`/`Sync`).** Goal: let example/user source run under bare CPython without `from tpy import Own` + `PYTHONPATH=lib/cpy`. Motivating file `examples/net/stream_server.py` (the `handle` coroutine is spawned by `start_server`, so its `StreamReader`/`StreamWriter` params genuinely need `Own` -- a borrow would dangle; the annotation is load-bearing, not decorative). Three complementary mechanisms, designed via `/tpy-add-feature` (architectural, but collapses to **parser-only**). **Key invariant: all compiler-facing spellings normalize *in the parser* to the existing canonical `TpyTypeRef("tpy:<marker>", (inner,), loc)` -- the exact node `Own[T]` emits today -- so sema/type_resolver/typesys/codegen need ZERO changes.** Single chokepoint verified: `_parse_subscript_type_ref` (`tpyc/parse/parser.py:2925`, marker-name->canonical-ref) + `TypeResolver.resolve` dispatch (`tpyc/parse/type_resolver.py:355-401`). Mechanisms: **(1) `typing.Annotated`** -- `Annotated[T, Own]` (imported sentinel; PEP 593 makes type-checkers see `T`, refactor-safe) and `Annotated[T, "own"]` (string marker; 100% stdlib, zero tpy import). Intercept `Annotated[T, m...]` in `_parse_subscript_type_ref`: inner `T` is the type; metadata that are tpy markers (sentinel or string `own`/`readonly`/`send`/`sync`) wrap `T` in the canonical ref; non-marker metadata ignored per PEP 593; nests (`Span[Annotated[T,"readonly"]]`) -- resolver's `_refloat_markers` handles canonical ordering. Generalizes to all four markers. Recommend accepting BOTH sentinel and string forms. **(2) Stub package** -- ship `lib/cpy/tpy` as installable `tpy` so `Own[T]` source runs under `python3` with no `PYTHONPATH`. Packaging-only (separable track): wheel currently ships `packages=["tpyc"]` (`name="tpy-lang"`, hatch); `lib/cpy` is only a pyright `extraPaths`. Decision needed: same `tpy-lang` wheel vs a separate `tpy-runtime` dist. Highest leverage -- covers ALL existing tpy-importing source, not just new spellings. **(3) per-param `# tpy:` comment** -- `reader: T  # tpy: own` keeps the annotation plain stdlib. Feasible: params built from `ast.arg` (`parser.py:2247-2283`), `arg.annotation` carries `.lineno`/`.end_lineno`, so map the trailing comment to the param; wrap its type in the canonical ref. Reuses the raw-line approach of `_scan_directives` (`parser.py:273`) at param granularity. Decision needed: require the marked param on its own line; reject 2 params sharing a line with a marker comment (clean diagnostic). Param-position only in v1 (returns/fields/locals deferred). Highest plumbing cost, narrowest benefit -- **(1)+(2) already fully solve the motivating case**, so (3) is optional polish. CPython parity: all three are runtime no-ops (Annotated metadata ignored, comments ignored, `Own` stub transparent) -> Match; no `no_cpython.txt`. No existing snapshots change (purely additive; TPy rejects `Annotated` today, so zero regression risk). Tests: per-spelling happy + nested + non-marker-ignored + multi-marker; comment one-per-line error + unknown-marker error. Docs: `LANGUAGE_FEATURES.md` (marker-spelling section, name `Own[T]` canonical with the rest as compat spellings) + `TPY_FOR_AGENTS.md`. Recommended build order: (1) Annotated -> (2) packaging -> (3) comment. **Adjacent cleanup:** no central marker-name registry -- the list is duplicated at `parser.py:2925` and `type_resolver.py:355`; the new string-marker map is a natural home to unify them.
- Named-constant (value-pattern) field comparison in class patterns: `case Dog(size=Size.BIG):` is rejected across all subject kinds (record, union, polymorphic) with a guard hint, while the literal form `case Dog(size=4):` works. Only *literal* field comparisons are emitted -- `_record_field_conditions` (`tpyc/codegen_cpp/match.py`) handles `TpyLiteralPattern` and union field guards, not `TpyValuePattern`; sema rejects value patterns in `MatchAnalyzer._resolve_class_pattern_fields` (`tpyc/sema/match.py`). Workaround: a guard (`case Dog() if d.size == Size.BIG:`). Fix: lower the value-pattern's dotted-name expression (enum member / module constant) and emit `__case.size == <value>`, mirroring the literal branch -- one site in `_record_field_conditions` plus dropping the sema rejection; benefits record/union/polymorphic uniformly since they share that helper. Also extend the dedup/exhaustiveness key (`MatchAnalyzer._field_constraint_key`) to fold the value constraint, as the literal branch already does. Surfaced finishing literal field-value matching.
- Property narrowing not supported: `if f.val is not None: f.val + 1` does not narrow because property access is a method call (could return different values). Same as function calls. Workaround: `v = f.val; if v is not None: v + 1`. Could support narrowing for simple field-returning getters in the future (requires getter body analysis).
- Tuple iteration + Iterable conformance bundle. `for x in some_tuple:` fails sema with "Cannot iterate over type tuple[...]", and passing a tuple (literal or bound variable) to an `Iterable[T]` param fails conformance in `sema/protocols.py`. Real CPython compat gap -- `math.prod((1.0, 2.0, 3.0))` works in CPython, errors in TPy. Coupled fix needed: (1) sema `type_conforms_to_protocol` case for TupleType when all element types widen to T; (2) codegen emits a std::array-style view from `std::tuple<T, T, ...>` for iteration (std::tuple has no begin/end); (3) for-loop codegen dispatches to that view for tuple iterables. Doing just (1) would make sema accept code that then fails C++ template instantiation (the class of bug we've been cleaning up). Workaround: bind to `list[T]` first or pass a list literal. Surfaced while making math.prod/fsum/sumprod/dist take Iterable[float]; those are now done but skip tuple inputs.
- No `AnyInt` protocol covering BigInt + all `AnyFixedInt`. `AnyFixedInt` bounds a generic to fixed-width ints only (excludes BigInt). Writing a generic stdlib function like `gcd[T: AnyInt](a: T, b: T) -> T` that accepts BigInt *and* any fixed-width int has no natural bound. Surfaces when trying to write pure-TPy generics over int types (`math.gcd`, `math.isqrt`, anywhere int-algebraic). Workaround: declare as `int`-only (forces BigInt; small-int callers pay widening cost). Fix: add an `AnyInt` protocol in `lib/tpy/tpy/_core/_types.py` that unions BigInt + AnyFixedInt, with the arithmetic/comparison surface needed for common algorithms.
- Generic type params over int types can't use basic arithmetic without a bound. Pure-TPy `def gcd[T](a: T, b: T) -> T` rejects `a = -a` ("Cannot reassign parameter"; workaround: copy to local), `x = -x` on T ("Invalid operand type for unary '-'"), and `abs(x)` on T ("No matching overload for abs(T)"). Each is because T has no known signed-int bound. Resolving via the `AnyInt` protocol above (with `__neg__` / `__abs__` surface) would unblock pure-TPy integer generics. The ban on reassigning generic-typed parameters is probably separate and may warrant its own review -- for some patterns it's a real ergonomic tax.
- No `Numeric` (or `AnyNumber`) protocol covering `AnyInt + float + Float32`. Blocks writing a generic `math.prod[T: Numeric](Iterable[T], *, start: T = 1) -> T` and similar generic numerics. `start: T = 1` works at sema (int literal coerces to T via normal default-value coercion; verified), so the only missing piece is the bound itself plus its declared arithmetic surface (`__mul__`, `__add__` at minimum). Depends on `AnyInt` above. Once both land, `prod` can be a single generic instead of N overloads, avoiding the empty-list W2 ambiguity entirely. Broader payoff: unblocks user-written generic numeric code (vector math, statistics fragments, aggregation primitives) that currently has no bound to use.
- Atomic shared-ownership smart pointer in TPy: `Arc[T]` (atomic refcounts). Non-atomic `Rc[T]` and its `Weak[T]` companion ship in `tplib` for single-threaded use; the multi-threaded analog is a v3+ item (multi-threaded executor, multi-awaiter Future). Open design: refcount-update memory ordering (acquire/release vs seq-cst on each refcount op), `Weak[T]`'s atomic upgrade compare-exchange shape, and interaction with cross-thread async primitives + a `Sync` bound on T.
- `thread_local` storage in TPy. v1.2 step 7 collapsed the C++-side `executor_ops` global entirely; the only remaining shared async state is `_current_executor: Ptr[Executor]` in `lib/tpy/asyncio/_executor.py`, also a plain (single-process) global. The TLS upgrade is gated on multi-threaded async (v3+) actually landing; until then `thread_local` is purely a future-proofing item, not a porting blocker.
- TPy method bodies on `@native` classes. Today the compiler enforces "methods on @native classes must have '...' body" (`tpyc/sema/analyzer.py:1624`). Constructors via `@overload @cpp_template` work, but instance methods are stub-only. v1.2 step 7 deleted both `Waker` and `ExecutorHandle` from C++ (Waker is now pure TPy, ExecutorHandle is gone), so the original async-side motivation is moot. The gap is still real for other `@native` types that might want TPy method bodies (none on the immediate roadmap). Open question for the design: how the TPy body resolves field access on a C++-owned struct, and whether to allow mixing stub methods + body methods on the same class.
- `imported_protocol_qualification` (`tpyc/typesys.py`) consults `ProtocolInfo.module` (the public re-export module), while the record-side analog uses `RecordInfo.defining_module` (the actual private submodule). If a `@dynamic` protocol ever lives in a private submodule re-exported under a different public name (the `tpy._builtins._bytes` -> `tpy` pattern records use), `qualified_cpp_name` would emit the wrong namespace for cross-module refs. Currently safe because no `@dynamic` protocol uses that re-export pattern; add `defining_module` to `ProtocolInfo` if/when one does. Surfaced by /tpy-review of v1.2 step 7.
- `self.x: T = value` field declarations in `__init__` (audit B127). The single most common Python field-declaration idiom is rejected with an opaque "Invalid assignment target" (the parser refuses any annotated non-Name target); TPy forces the class-level-annotation + bare-assign split. Design approved in outline: accept in `__init__` only -- the annotation declares the field (equivalent to the class-level annotation) or validates against an existing one; conflicting types error; outside `__init__` stays rejected with a message pointing at both spellings. Needs `/tpy-add-feature` (field-registration interplay, MIL-init ordering). All four shapes rejected today: scalar + container, with and without the class-level annotation. Split out of the parser-fidelity cluster fix per approval.
- `NoReturn` return-type support. The missing-return check (a non-None return type must terminate on every path) cannot see that a trailing call never returns -- `def f() -> Int32: ...; sys.exit(1)` is rejected even though the call cannot fall through, because TPy has no `NoReturn` type to declare on `sys.exit` / panic wrappers (`lib/tpy/sys.py` carries the matching TODO). Workaround: end with `raise` or an `assert False`. Fix shape: accept `NoReturn` as a return annotation, treat a call to a `NoReturn` function as a terminator in `liveness.stmts_terminate`, and declare it on `sys.exit` and the raise-wrapper stubs. Surfaced implementing the missing-return check.
- Match guard lowering still has three open-coded copies of the shape the shared helpers capture: `_emit_optional_inner_record` / `_emit_optional_inner_if_elif` fold guards into the arm condition instead of routing through `_gen_match_optional_cond` + `_emit_guarded_arm_tail` (safe today only because `_partition_optional_cases` bails guard+binding arms to the fixed chain), and `_gen_match_polymorphic_guarded` open-codes the guard-tail (guard-if + goto + close). Unify all three onto the helpers so the next pattern kind can't reintroduce the severed-chain/binding-after-guard bug class; while there, replace the lone `getattr(case.pattern, "binds_full_optional", False)` read with typed access. All in `tpyc/codegen_cpp/match.py`. Surfaced by /tpy-review of the match-lowering cluster fix.
- `collect_type_args_of_bounded_records` (`tpyc/codegen_cpp/protocols.py:740`) still has an `info.builtin_type_key is None` guard that its sibling `collect_record_types_from_type` dropped in v1.2 step 7. The two helpers now treat in-module `@builtin_type` records inconsistently: if `Waker` (or any other `@builtin_type` record) ever appears as a type arg to a bounded record, the second helper would silently exclude it. Align the two to the same policy. Surfaced by /tpy-review of v1.2 step 7.
- Generic class specializations -- closed by v1.2 step 5 (Poll[T] port). The premise that `Poll[T]` needed a TPy-level specialization mechanism turned out to be wrong: a single `@nocopy class Poll[T]` body with `UninitArrayStorage[T, 1] + bool` storage covers the value-T / void / reference-T / move-only / non-default-constructible cases by composition of existing primitives. The void analog rides on `Poll[None]` -> `Poll<std::monostate>` lowering, the reference-T specialization (`Poll<T&>` in the old C++) was never instantiated by generated code, and `UninitArrayStorage`'s placement-new contract handles move-only / non-default-constructible T identically to `std::optional<T>`. Specialization syntax may still be useful for a future feature, but it's not blocking anything in the async pipeline; left as a research item rather than a tracked TODO.
- `@cpp_template` literal-brace escape syntax. The substitution engine treats `{...}` as a placeholder field name, so C++ aggregate-init / lambda / scope braces in the template string can't be expressed (e.g. `@cpp_template("::tpy::Handle{static_cast<void*>(&{0})}")` fails with `Internal error: unexpected '{' in field name`). Workaround today: wrap the brace-using expression in a templated C++ helper and bind to that helper via `@cpp_template`. Fix: add `{{` / `}}` Python-f-string-style escape syntax. Pairs with the BUGS.md entry on the same code path (UX: produce a clear diagnostic instead of an internal error when literal braces appear). Surfaced during async v1.1 Phase 2.2.
- `Final[list[T]]` / `Final[Array[T, N]]` for compile-time constant tables. Today `Final[T]` only allows primitives (`int`, `float`, `bool`, `str`/`StrView`, `Char`, `IntN`) and `tuple` (see `docs/LANGUAGE_FEATURES.md:5071`); array-shaped round-constant tables (SHA-2 H0/K, AES S-boxes, CRC tables, etc.) have no natural spelling. `Final[tuple[UInt32, UInt32, ..., UInt32]]` works but is unwieldy at N=64+ and doesn't compose with `Span[UInt32]` consumers without a conversion. A module-level `list[UInt32]` heap-allocates once at module init; correct but wrong shape for what is conceptually a `constexpr std::array`. Right surface is probably `Final[Array[UInt32, 8]]` -> `inline constexpr std::array<uint32_t, 8>` (header-emitted, no init order, fully constant-folded). Open: whether to also accept `Final[list[T]]` as syntactic sugar that codegens to the same `std::array` (size from initializer length).
- `Stringable` structural protocol + binop acceptance. Any type with `__str__ -> StrView` would conform; `__add__` / `__eq__` / etc. on string-like types (`str`, `String`, `StrView`, plus user types like the Pascal POC's `PStr[N]`) could declare `other: Stringable` once instead of duplicating overloads per concrete operand type. Today the convention is explicit overloads -- see `str.__add__` in `lib/tpy/tpy/_builtins/_types.py:453-464` for the four-way overload set (`str`/`String`/`StrView`/`Char`) -- and a user record like `PStr` must repeat the pattern or force translator-side `str(...)` wrapping at call sites (Pascal POC takes the latter route in `frontends/pascal/pascal_translator/translate.py::_coerce_string_operand`). Open design: (a) structural protocol definition with the `__str__ -> StrView` requirement; (b) whether sema's binop-dispatch / argument-coercion path should auto-`__str__` an operand when the formal type is `Stringable` (consistent with how protocols are normally satisfied -- *not* a special-cased implicit coercion); (c) interaction with `Char`, which has a string-conversion via `tpy::char_to_str` rather than `__str__`. Non-issue today but cleans up several stdlib overload duplications + lets out-of-tree user string types (PStr / FixStr / future Pascal Char-string / future C-string wrappers) state their operand surface once.
- `Out[T]` annotation for by-ref out-parameters (upstream #20). C/C++ APIs commonly return multiple values via by-reference out-params (`void f(Key k, FooBuf*& outFoo, BarBuf*& outBar)`). Porting these to TPy today requires changing the signature shape -- typically a `tuple[Ptr[T], Ptr[T]]` return -- which deviates from source parity. Surface: `def f(k: Key, out_foo: Out[Ptr[FooBuf]], out_bar: Out[Ptr[BarBuf]]) -> None`, lowering to `void f(Key k, FooBuf*& out_foo, BarBuf*& out_bar)`. Two call-site shapes to consider: (a) implicit pack -- `foo, bar = f(k)` and the compiler synthesizes locals bound by reference to the out positions; (b) explicit -- caller declares locals first and passes by name (`f(k, out_foo=foo, out_bar=bar)`). Body-side semantics in both cases: assignments to `Out[T]` parameters write through to the caller's locals. Why it matters: a common codegen shape for helpers that produce more than one value -- with `Out[T]` the port can stay 1:1 with the C++ source. Existing parity gap (recap): tpy already special-cases container parameters to be passed as `T&` / `const T&` based on mutation analysis; `Out[T]` extends that to a user-facing annotation for the case where the caller explicitly wants by-ref semantics regardless of mutation.
- Focused regression test: `@cpp_template` free function taking `Own[T]` of a `@nocopy` type, called with a local at its last use. Should pin the auto-move emission (snapshot shows `std::move(local)` in the expansion). Currently verified only transitively via async tests; a focused test would make the contract explicit and catch regressions cleanly. See v1.2 step 2 in `docs/ASYNC_PROGRESS.md`.
- Regression test for the BUGS "borrow protocol passed to consuming-only sink" entry: an `error_awaitable_non_own_consuming/` case with `# tpyc: error(/...)` would lock in the failure mode and become a regression target when the sema-diagnostic fix lands.
- Regression test for `await asyncio.create_task(sub())` in **discard-result** position (no `result =` binding). The existing `tests/cases/async/await_task_rvalue/` case covers the bind-result form of the ERASED-mode rvalue path; the discard form takes a different codegen branch (no var assignment, just `.reset()` and state transition). Low-priority pin since the underlying mechanism is shared with bind-result, but the codegen paths diverge enough that an explicit test would catch any future drift between them.
- Regression test for `await failed_task` where the task raised a non-`CancelledError` exception. Documents the current slicing behavior (`BaseException` is re-raised, not the concrete subclass) and would catch any future TaskState-exception fix that preserves the dynamic type. Tied to the BUGS entry on "Caught BaseException as e stored into a field slices the dynamic type" -- the test pins the surface symptom.
- Coverage gap: borrow-tracker tests (`tests/cases/pointers/warn_borrow_ptr/`, `tests/cases/pointers/warn_borrow_field_path/`) only exercise the explicit `take_ptr(x)` path. The direct value-to-`Ptr[T]` coercion path (`p: Ptr[T] = x`) now registers the same `PTR` borrow + non-null provenance via the `coerce_expr` mutation hook in `sema/compatibility.py` and the `expr_yields_non_null_ptr` helper in `sema/context.py`, but those wirings have no dedicated regression test. Add `warn_borrow_direct_coercion/` mirroring the take_ptr shape with `p: Ptr[Point] = point` initializers and a rebind line (`p = other_point`) -- the rebind path consumes the same helper but lives at a separate site so a single-site refactor that drops one call can't silently shrink borrow coverage. Surfaced by `/tpy-review` of the value-to-Ptr coercion cleanup.
- Silent-warning gap in `tests/cases/tuple/tuple_optional_ptr_mixed/`. After the value-to-Ptr coercion now fires the mutation signal, the case emits a new "Mutation while borrowed" warning in `diag.txt` but the `main.py` source has no `# tpyc: warning(/.../)` annotation pinning it. Snapshots will keep passing because the diagnostic is captured, but the annotation-validator can't confirm the warning is intentional and a future regression that silently drops the warning would only fail at snapshot-diff level, not at the annotation level. Add the `# tpyc: warning(...)` marker on the offending line so the test's intent is explicit. Surfaced by `/tpy-review` of the value-to-Ptr coercion cleanup.
- Coverage expansion for pointer-form generator/coro frame slots (post `fix-coro-frame-optional-nonvalue`). Four missing variants: (a) standalone state-machine sync generator over `list[NonValue]` with multi-yield body to isolate the pointer-form loop var from the tuple-yield shape in `tests/cases/tuple/tuple_optional_yield_prev/`; (b) async coroutine with a `for x in list[NonValue]: await ...` body (non-Optional element) -- exercises the CFG-decomposed for-loop frame path; (c) generator-method variant on a class with `def gen(self) -> Iterator[...]` returning pointer-form data -- covers the `record_name != None` branch of `_gen_generator_code`; (d) async coroutine method with `Optional[NonValue]` local across an await -- covers the `record_name != None` branch of `_gen_coro_code`. Surfaced by `/tpy-review` of the coro-frame-optional-nonvalue fix.
- Pre-existing TpyCall non-null predicate duplicated at two sites that the `expr_yields_non_null_ptr` consolidation didn't fold in: `tpyc/sema/statements.py:2925-2941` (borrow-registration site -- repeats the `init.call_type.is_pointer() or fi.value_ptr_coercion` test plus a hardcoded coercion-name list that's now redundant with `Coercion.produces_non_null_ptr`) and `tpyc/sema/compatibility.py:1693-1697` (dangling-return classification -- same TpyCall predicate). Fold both into the helper (or a sibling that returns the discriminating call info) so a future change to "what counts as a Ptr-yielding call" lives in one place. Out of scope for the original refactor; surfaced by `/simplify` review of the value-to-Ptr coercion cleanup.
- Coverage gap: `Diagnostic.format` in `tpyc/diagnostics.py` now prefers `loc.file` over the caller-supplied fallback name (so a frontend plugin's diagnostics point into the DSL source, not the generated `.py`), but no test exercises the `loc.file`-set path. Add a `tpyc/`-level unit test on `Diagnostic.format` with a location carrying its own file (toolchain-free), or a frontend-plugin error case that surfaces a `.pas`-file location. Surfaced by `/tpy-review` of the enum-field-default branch.
- Coverage gap: enum-member field default honoring a `@native` member rename. `_render_enum_member_default` (`tpyc/codegen_cpp/records.py`) maps the member through `einfo.cpp_member_name_map`, but the happy-path test only uses pure-Python enums whose member names pass through unchanged. Add a case with a `@native` enum whose member has a C++-name override used as a field default, to pin the rename emission. Surfaced by `/tpy-review` of the enum-field-default branch.
- Coverage gap: cross-module *bad* enum member as a field default. The happy path covers cross-module enum defaults and the error path covers a same-module bad member, but no case names a non-existent member of an *imported* enum (`state: RemoteState = RemoteState.NONEXISTENT`). Add an error case to pin the registration-time rejection when the enum is cross-module. Surfaced by `/tpy-review` of the enum-field-default branch.
- **CPython-shape heterogeneous variadic-tuple `gather`**. v1.5 M9 ships two homogeneous entrypoints -- `asyncio.gather(*tasks: Task[T]) -> list[T]` (variadic-positional) and `asyncio.gather_list(tasks: list[Task[T]]) -> list[T]` (list shape) -- both requiring all tasks to share return type `T`. The CPython-shape *heterogeneous* form `gather[*Ts](*coros: Awaitable[*Ts]) -> tuple[*Ts]` requires variadic generics (TPy doesn't have them) plus the async-def `*args` codegen fix in BUGS.md. When both prerequisites land, the heterogeneous form coexists with the homogeneous shapes as an overload of `gather` (distinguished by element-type uniformity). Until then, mixed-type concurrent fetches use the `create_task` + `await task` pattern.
- **Star-unpack into varargs: container cover-set is list/span/array only.** `*xs` into a vararg pack (`_unpack_star_element_type` in `tpyc/sema/expressions.py`, and the parallel validation in `_analyze_and_pack_varargs`) accepts only directly-iterable lvalue containers: `list`, `Span`, `Array`, `PendingListType`. CPython permits unpacking any iterable -- `tuple`, `set`, `bytes`, `str`, generators, dict views. This gap is language-wide (affects non-generic varargs too: `sum_all(*tuple)` rejects identically), not specific to the generic path. Expanding it means deciding which iterables codegen can lower into `tpy::varargs<T>` and threading the element-type extraction + the `as_mut_span`-equivalent for each. Separate feature, not a defect. Surfaced during `fix-star-unpack-generic-vararg`.
- **Star-unpack into variadic builtins (`print(*xs)`) is rejected, not spread.** `print(*xs)`, and any other variadic *builtin* with a special-cased handler, currently rejects `*unpack` with the `analyze_expr` safety-net diagnostic ("the call target does not accept *args") rather than spreading the elements. User-defined variadic functions/methods support `*list` unpack (the `fix-star-unpack-generic-vararg` work); the builtin handlers (`print`, etc. in `tpyc/sema/calls.py`'s builtin-dispatch paths) don't route their arg analysis through the vararg-pack machinery, so spread isn't wired for them. CPython users naturally write `print(*items)`. Supporting it means teaching each variadic builtin's handler to accept/lower a star-unpack (or routing builtins through the shared vararg packer). Feature, not a defect -- the current behavior is a clean reject. Surfaced during `fix-star-unpack-generic-vararg`.
- **[feature] Mixed positional + star-unpack in one vararg call (`f(a, *xs, b)`, `f(*a, *b)`).** CPython accepts arbitrary interleaving of positionals and unpacks in a call (PEP 448): `sum_all(1, *mid, 2)` -> `sum_all(1, 10, 20, 2)`; `f(*a, *b)` concatenates. TPy currently requires a `*xs` unpack to be the *sole* entry in the vararg region and rejects the mix at sema with "Cannot mix *unpacking with other arguments" (`_analyze_and_pack_varargs` in `tpyc/sema/calls.py`; regression test `tests/cases/calls/error_star_unpack_mixed/`). The reject replaced a *silent miscompile* (codegen's `_gen_vararg_pack` at `tpyc/codegen_cpp/expressions.py:5308` `return`s on the first `TpyStarUnpack`, dropping sibling args -- `sum_all(1, *mid, 2)` gave 30 not 33). This is a divergence from CPython, not a hard limit; the reject is the correct interim.

  **Why it's not a quick patch:** `_gen_vararg_pack` has only two shapes today -- a fixed `std::array<T*, N>`/`std::array<T, N>` for individual args (compile-time N), and a direct `varargs<T>(as_mut_span(xs))` for a lone unpack. A mix has a **runtime** length (the unpack's size is dynamic), so neither shape fits. Supporting it needs:
  1. **Runtime** (`runtime/cpp/include/tpy/varargs.hpp`): a `varargs<T>` constructor over a runtime-sized pointer buffer (it is already dual-mode with an `indirect_` `T* const*`; add a dynamic-size entry, e.g. from `std::span<T* const>` / `const std::vector<T*>&`, and the value-type analogue).
  2. **Codegen**: assemble a runtime buffer -- push leading positionals (`&tmp`), append each unpacked container's elements (loop / `insert(end, span.begin(), span.end())`), push trailing positionals -- then `varargs<T>(buffer)`. Handle value-vs-ref element modes and multiple unpacks.
  3. **Lifetime (the awkward bit):** the buffer must be a **hoisted statement-level local**, NOT a GCC statement-expression (`({...; value;})`) -- the `varargs` holds pointers into the buffer, so if the buffer dies at the end of the statement-expression the view dangles. The literal-positional temps (`1`, `2`) must likewise outlive the call. This needs the codegen to emit prep statements before the call rather than a single initializer expression.

  Estimate ~0.5-1 day, medium risk (the buffer-lifetime pattern). Do it as its own scoped branch, not bolted onto star-unpack fixes. Surfaced by `/tpy-review` of `fix-star-unpack-generic-vararg`.
- **Star-unpack of an owning-rvalue / wrapped container is rejected.** `*make_items()` where the factory returns `Own[list[T]]` (or any `Own[...]`/`Ref[...]`-wrapped container) is cleanly rejected at sema with "Cannot unpack type 'Own[list[...]]' into *args" rather than supported. Earlier attempt to peel the wrapper in `_unpack_star_element_type` made sema accept it, but codegen then emitted `tpy::as_mut_span(<rvalue>)` which fails to compile (as_mut_span needs an lvalue span/vector reference, not a temporary). Supporting this needs codegen to materialize the owning rvalue into a frame/stack temp and span over that before packing. Workaround: bind to a local first (`xs = make_items(); f(*xs)`). Surfaced during `fix-star-unpack-generic-vararg`.
- **Double `analyze_expr(arg.expr)` for generic-vararg star-unpack.** A generic vararg call with `*xs` analyzes the inner container twice -- once in `_infer_arg_types` (inference) and again in `_analyze_and_pack_varargs` (packing). The shared `ExpressionAnalyzer.analyze_call_arg` helper unified the *logic* but not the two-pass structure. Harmless today (the second `analyze_expr` is a cached scope lookup for the common `TpyName` shape), but fragile if expression analysis ever gains non-idempotent side effects (borrow-tracking entries, flow-fact merge counters). Fix: have the packing pass consult the inference pass's cached type rather than re-analyze. Surfaced by `/code-review` of `fix-star-unpack-generic-vararg`.
- **[architectural cleanup] Unify call-argument analysis into one entry point.** Call arguments are analyzed at ~12+ scattered sites across `tpyc/sema/calls.py` and `methods.py` (generic-fn inference, the two overload-probe regimes, method-subst inference, pending-container/pending-generic-method paths, the per-builtin handlers, constructor fallbacks), each iterating `expr.args` and calling `analyze_expr` directly with no shared chokepoint that knows the callee signature. The `fix-star-unpack-generic-vararg` work reached *correct* star-unpack behavior but via a defense-in-depth patchwork rather than a design: (a) a `ExpressionAnalyzer.analyze_call_arg` helper that only the variadic-capable sites opt into; (b) a default-reject for `TpyStarUnpack` in `analyze_expr` as a *safety net* covering every site that doesn't opt in; (c) the "does not accept *args" reject duplicated at 3-4 sites (free-fn `_typecheck_call_args`, the generic non-variadic loop, the method dispatch, plus the analyze_expr net); (d) the variadic-element unwrap (`unwrap_readonly(unwrap_ref_type(span).type_args[0])`) now a 3rd copy of a pattern also in `type_ops.seed_arg_hint` / `infer_type_params_for_function`. A from-scratch design would have a single `analyze_call_args(expr, callee_signature)` that routes every call path, handling star-unpack per-position (element-extract at a variadic slot, reject otherwise) in one place -- which would let the safety-net reject and the duplicated gates be deleted, and would subsume the double-analyze item above. **This is NOT THIR-migration work**: call-arg analysis runs entirely in sema before THIR lowering, so the migration won't dissolve the scatter; folding this cleanup into THIR would only bloat it. Do it as its own deliberately-scoped sema refactor branch starting from the unified design, not as incremental edits. Surfaced by the multi-round review of `fix-star-unpack-generic-vararg`.
- **`_extract_awaitable_inner` doesn't substitute INT-kind type-params.** The fix at `tpyc/sema/expressions.py:_extract_awaitable_inner` (feat-gather-variadic) routes through `_substitute_type_params` from `tpyc/sema/operators.py`, which walks via `map_inner_types`. `NominalType.inner_types()` filters out INT-kind type-args, so a poll-shape carrying an INT type-param (e.g. `class MyAwait[T, N: int]: def __poll__(self, w) -> Own[Poll[Array[T, N]]]`) would leave `N` unsubstituted. No existing user has this shape; latent until a future generic awaitable carries an INT type param. Fix: replace the operators-side helper with `TypeOps.substitute_type_params` (handles INT slots explicitly) at the await-extraction site. Surfaced by `/code-review` of feat-gather-variadic.
- **Non-asyncio positive test for recursive `_extract_awaitable_inner` substitution.** The `tpyc/sema/expressions.py:_extract_awaitable_inner` fix that makes `list[T]` (and other nested generic shapes) substitute properly at the await-result-type inference site is currently only exercised by `tests/cases/async/asyncio_gather_varargs/`. A user-defined class with `def __poll__(self, w: Waker) -> Own[Poll[list[T]]]` (or `Poll[tuple[T, T]]` etc.) outside the asyncio stack would give the sema fix independent regression coverage. ~20 LOC under `tests/cases/async/` or `tests/cases/generics/`. Surfaced by `/tpy-review` of feat-gather-variadic.
- **`asyncio.gather_list` `return_exceptions=True`**. CPython's gather optionally collects exceptions into the result list (`list[T | BaseException]`) instead of cancel-and-re-raise. v1.5 M9 ships the default-False semantics only. Adding the kwarg-True path needs Box[Throwable] storage + a union-typed return list; ~30 lines on `_GatherFuture[T]`.
- **`Task.clone()` shared-TaskState semantics (multi-awaiter Task design).** v1.5 M9 added `Task[T].clone()` as a cheap Rc-bump used internally by `_GatherFuture[T]`. The clone aliases the same TaskState as the original handle, but `TaskState` is single-awaiter (one `awaiter: Waker` slot overwritten by each `__poll__`; one cached result slot consumed on first observation). Two real failure modes follow from any user who holds both the original Task and a clone: (a) **multi-awaiter alias** -- awaiting both concurrently (or sequentially when the first awaiter already consumed the result) overwrites/panics the second; e.g. `tasks=[t]; await gather_list(tasks); await tasks[0]` panics on `RuntimeError('Task: __poll__ after Ready was already consumed')`; (b) **cancel leak via shared TaskState** -- `_GatherFuture._propagate_cancel` cancels its clone, which routes through `TaskState.cancel_any()` and marks the user's original handle as cancelled too, surprising callers who didn't ask. The internal `Task.clone()` docstring carries a warning; the proper fix is a multi-awaiter `TaskState` (additive `awaiter_count` + waker list; result slot becomes ref-counted multi-consumer or "first consumer wins" panic moved into a typed `result_consumed` error). v2 scale -- not v1.5. Surfaced by /code-review of feat-asyncio-gather.
- **`@contextlib.contextmanager`**. Real user-convenience shortcut for cleanup-only context managers (post sync-`with` upgrade to 4-arg `__exit__`). Generator + try/finally + decorator pattern -- TPy has all three primitives. Probably ~30 lines of stdlib code or a class macro. Closes the ergonomics gap that bare 4-arg `__exit__` leaves for the common "I just want cleanup" case.
- **[correctness-fragility] `desugar_suspensions` compound traversal is a hand-maintained allowlist.** `_recurse_sub_bodies` / `_lower_stmt_exprs` walk a statement's `fields()` and then special-case the compound shapes whose await-bearing positions don't live in plain expr / stmt-list fields (try-handlers, `match` arm bodies, `with`-item context exprs). The canonical `exprs()` / `sub_bodies()` interfaces would be the natural traversal but are read-only (can't write the lowered expr back), so the pass mirrors `gen_async._map_compound_subbodies` instead. Risk: if a NEW compound node or await-bearing position is added later and not added to this enumeration, the failure mode is a *silently un-lowered await in a conditional / repeated position* -- the eval-order bug class this pass exists to fix, resurfacing in an unenumerated spot with no diagnostic. Fix: a writable visitor (map-with-rebuild over the canonical child interface) so await positions can't be missed by omission. Not urgent (enumeration is complete for today's node set); flagged so it isn't mistaken for cosmetic. Surfaced by the /tpy-ready retrospective second opinion.
- **`desugar_suspensions._has_await` re-scans subtrees.** `_lower` calls `_has_await(expr)` up front, then the per-construct branches call `_has_await` again on operands already known to contain a suspension (O(n^2) in chained-compare operand count / nesting depth). Not a practical cliff at current sizes, but folds out by threading a "contains-suspension" result down instead of re-scanning. Surfaced by /tpy-review (architecture-fit) of the await eval-order fix.
- **`await` inside a comprehension / generator expression**. Currently rejected cleanly by the pre-sema suspension desugar (`tpyc/parse/desugar_suspensions.py`) -- the `(prefix, value)` linearizer model doesn't fit a per-iteration suspension inside the comprehension's sub-scope. Full support means desugaring the comprehension to an explicit accumulation loop (`result = []; for x in xs: result.append(await f(x))`) before sema, including the `async for` comprehension form (`[x async for x in ait]`). Distinct from the eval-order desugar (which handles `and`/`or`/ternary/chained-compare/`while`-condition); this is a separate desugar-to-loop. Tests: `tests/cases/async/error_await_in_comprehension` guards the current reject.
- **[LOW prio, optimization] Share coro-frame sub-future slots via one variant.** Today each `await` site allocates its own `std::optional<__coro_X> __sub_N` frame field (`gen_async.py::_emit_resumable_sub_future_fields`), so a function with N awaits carries N inline sub-coro slots even though they are never live simultaneously -- e.g. `await num() or await num() ...` (4 short-circuit lines) emits `__sub_0`..`__sub_7`, all `std::optional<__coro_num>` (see `tests/cases/async/await_short_circuit/expected/include/main.hpp`). Invariant that makes sharing sound: **at most one `__sub` is engaged per frame at any time** -- a coroutine parks at exactly one suspension point, and codegen resets the in-flight sub-future before advancing (and on the catch path); `create_task`/`gather` hold their own Rc/heap storage, not the caller's `__sub`. So one slot is the theoretical minimum and no liveness/interval analysis is needed. Cleanest shape: a single per-frame `std::variant<std::monostate, __coro_A, __coro_B, ...>` over the *distinct* sub-coro types (`monostate` = idle); the state machine knows the active type at each resume case, so polling is a static `std::get<__coro_X>(__sub).poll(waker)` (no `std::visit`, no dispatch cost), emplace via `__sub.emplace<__coro_X>(args)`, reset via `__sub.emplace<std::monostate>()`. The all-same-type case degenerates to a single-alternative variant (~optional). Caveats: dedup the alternative list (variant rejects duplicate types); slot size becomes max-alternative + tag (fine -- one slot is the floor); decide whether erased/boxed awaits (`await task` -> `unique_ptr<AsyncFrameBase<U>>`, a separate slot kind, also <=1-live) fold in as a pointer alternative or share one boxed slot; and do NOT fold in result slots (`__r_*` / hoisted locals) or `_lift_borrowed_rvalue_args` temps -- those genuinely overlap and stay separate. Recursive-async sizing is unchanged (same alternatives inlined as the optionals were). This is the concrete form of `docs/ASYNC_DESIGN.md` Open Question 6. Benefits both `async def` and (post-migration) generators -- but generators have no sub-futures, so async-only in practice. Frame-size win for await-heavy functions; not correctness.
- **Async borrow-checker hardening**. Two related lifetime gaps surfaced by the v1.5 control-flow lift (BUGS.md 202-205). Gap 1: `create_task(f(local_ref))` where `f` has reference-typed params captures by reference and the Task escapes the caller's scope -- dangling. Gap 2: borrowed-mode `await` on a temporary or short-lived reference-type awaitable. M3 (await-in-control-flow) ships with cheap mitigations: `_is_stable_lvalue` tightened to frame-fields-and-params only, and a sema warning on the escaping-create_task pattern. The real escape analysis (upgrade warnings to errors with proper call-graph tracking) is its own milestone, scheduled for after v1.5 completes (post-M6).
- **Runtime `type()` for exceptions**. v1.5's `__exit__(self, exc_type, exc_val, exc_tb) -> bool` always passes `exc_type = None` (sema rejects `exc_type is/== X` comparisons with a diagnostic pointing at `isinstance(exc_val, X)`). Real implementation needs class objects as first-class TPy values with a subclass-relationship table -- substantial sub-design touching the type system, runtime, and exception machinery. Unblocks: `exc_type is X` comparisons, `issubclass(exc_type, X)`, plus broader use of `type(x)` as a callable. Track as a separate language design item.
- **`@nomove` cost-refinement for inline-buffer types.** (The `@nomove` decorator itself now exists -- Phase 2 -- as "explicit non-movable": it sets `is_movable=False` so any relocation errors. What remains is the *cost-based* refinement below, for types that ARE movable but whose O(N) move the author wants made explicit.) Types with inline storage parameterized by N (`ArrayList[T, N]`, `Array[T, N]`, `FixStr[N]`) move in O(N) -- full element-by-element relocation, unlike `std::vector` whose move is a pointer-steal. Users who instantiate at large N can pay surprising costs at every implicit move site (return, `Own[T]` rebind, container reallocation). Surface: `@nomove class ArrayList[T, N]` declares "implicit moves not allowed; user must opt in via `copy()` at the call site -- be honest about the O(N) cost." Distinct from the `__move__` dunder (now Working): `__move__` *enables* an implicit relocating move (the O(N) cost is paid silently at return/reassign); `@nomove` is the opposite opt-out for an author who wants that cost made explicit via `copy()`. The two can coexist (a type whose move mechanism exists but whose author forbids implicit use). Open design points: (1) **NRVO-style elision** -- factory functions (`def make() -> ArrayList[T, N]: return ArrayList[T, N]()`) must still work; needs sema modeling of guaranteed copy/move elision so the trivial factory shape isn't rejected. This handling generalizes beyond `@nomove`; existing factory code benefits. (2) **Container storage** -- `list[NoMove]` reallocates via moves; needs a clean diagnostic pointing at the natural fix (`list[Box[NoMove]]`). (3) **Relation to `@nocopy`** -- orthogonal: a `@nomove` type can still be copy-constructible via `__copy__`, and `@nocopy` types like Box/Rc remain cheaply movable (pointer-steal). (4) **Diagnostic clarity** -- "implicit move of `@nomove` type would copy N elements; use `copy(x)` to make this explicit, or wrap in `Box[T]` for cheap moves" -- mirrors the existing "may copy X into field" warning class.
- **[feature] Movability trait -- remaining relocation-site coverage (Phase 2 residuals).** Phase 2 shipped: movability as a propagated trait (`is_movable`, sibling to `is_send`/`is_sync`, with `RecordInfo.is_movable`/`move_override`/`has_move`, per-generic re-eval), a `why_not_movable` field-chain diagnostic (`tpyc/sema/move_chain.py`), the `@nomove` decorator (explicit, library-declared non-movable), codegen emitting `= delete` for non-generic non-movable `__del__` classes (so the class compiles; was a latent ill-formed member-wise move), and sema enforcement at by-value returns and `Own[tuple[...]]` per-element argument passing (via `require_movable` in `check_own_lvalue_into_own`). The compiler has ZERO knowledge of any specific library type: `UninitArrayStorage` is `@nomove` in its own stub (raw storage can't relocate itself), `ArrayList`/`FixStr` define `__move__`, and trivial fixed scratch buffers (e.g. `EpollReactor`'s epoll-output arrays) use the movable `Array[T, N]` rather than `UninitArrayStorage`. Consequence: an owner of `UninitArrayStorage` is non-movable for *any* element (no element-conditional free move) unless it defines `__move__`. Residuals: (1) **relocation-site coverage** -- plain `Own[T]` scalar call-arguments, last-use rebind (`q = p`), container insert, comprehension elements (a LIVE site since master's `owns_elements` comprehension-move landed), and tuple construction are NOT yet movability-checked in sema, so a non-movable type relocated there still surfaces as the raw C++ deleted-move error (for non-generic non-movable classes) or a deleted-`UninitArrayStorage`-move error (for generic instantiations). Wire `require_movable` into the remaining `is_auto_move_use` consumer sites, OR lift "this expr is a relocating move" into a single sema fact (e.g. `relocating_expr_ids`, parallel to `all_last_uses`) consumed by both the movability check and codegen's `std::move` emission -- the latter is the THIR-aligned end state (codegen stops independently deciding `_is_last_use_movable`). (2) **partial-drain leak** -- codegen unconditionally emits `<src>.__tpy_owned_ = false` after a `__move__` body and `_warn_unconsumed_own_params` blanket-skips `__move__`, so a user `__move__` that fails to drain every live slot silently leaks; consider verifying full drain or keeping the source dtor live for un-taken slots. (3) **indirect throw in `__move__`** -- a direct un-`try`-guarded `raise` in a `__move__` body is now a compile error (`_first_escaping_raise` in `registration.py`, `tests/cases/movability/error_move_raises`), but the move ctor is `noexcept` and `__move__` runs arbitrary user TPy, so an *indirect* throw (a callee that raises, an allocation) still yields a silent `std::terminate` with no definition-site diagnostic. Catching it needs nothrow tracking TPy lacks -- "warn on any call" is unworkable because the correct bodies call `relocate_from` (a call we can't distinguish as nothrow). Revisit if a `nothrow` marker / throw-propagation feature lands (or emit conditional noexcept). (4) **direct-call diagnostic** -- `x.__move__(y)` compiles under CPython but fails at C++ build with no TPy diagnostic; reject direct `__move__` calls in sema. (5) **`TriviallyRelocatable` bound + `relocate_trivial` intrinsic (general feature, deferred until a 2nd use case or perf pressure)** -- with `UninitArrayStorage` `@nomove`, every owner writes `__move__`, even for trivially-relocatable elements where it's a hand loop the optimizer should memcpy. A general compiler-derived `TriviallyRelocatable[T]` trait (NOT `ValueType` -- it must EXCLUDE BigInt and owned `std::string`; a conservative whitelist of fixed-width ints/bool/Char/float/Ptr/views/tuples-of-those) would let a library `relocate_trivial[T: TriviallyRelocatable](dst, src, n)` primitive replace those hand loops, and could also gate a future memcpy-movable storage. Reusable beyond movability (serialization, native interop). Also: the C++ `UninitArrayStorage` move is still conditionally available (`requires is_trivially_copyable`) -- harmless dead code now (TPy never moves it), but could be unconditionally `= delete` to match the `@nomove` contract once the unchecked relocation sites in (1) are covered. (`has_move` on `RecordInfo`, also deferred from the Phase-1 review, shipped with Phase 2.) Inheritance: `__move__` on a class with a base that needs its own relocation is not yet handled (currently the trait walks parents structurally; explicit base-move chaining is untested). (6) **`@nomove` + `__move__` contradiction** -- a class declaring both is not rejected; codegen emits the custom `__move__` move ctor but suppresses move-assign (`nonmovable` gate), leaving it move-constructible-but-not-assignable (C++ Rule-of-5 implicit delete). Unreachable today (no class declares both). Reject the combination in sema: `__move__` *is* a movability declaration, so it contradicts `@nomove`. Surfaced building Phase 2.
- **[cleanup, now unblocked by `__move__`] Dogfood `UninitStorage[T]` as a pure-TPy class.** The single-slot owning storage (`tpy::UninitStorage<T>`, a `@native` C++ primitive) is now expressible in TPy: a `@nocopy` class over the `@nomove` `UninitArrayStorage[T, 1]` plus a liveness bool, with `__del__` and a `__move__` that transfers the live slot via `relocate_from(other._storage, 1)` -- correct for a non-trivially-relocatable payload, with the same nothrow constraint `relocate_from` already asserts. Before `__move__` this couldn't be written (no way to make a movable single-slot owner in TPy), so it's a genuine validation of the trait's generality. NOT worth doing casually: the consumers are the most ABI/bootstrap-sensitive primitives -- the `Rc` cell payload (`tplib/rc.py`, which caches a `Ptr` into `storage.ptr()` and is a generic `@dynamic` heap type with a careful layout), `Poll[T]` (`tpy/_core/_types.py`, one of the earliest-compiled modules -> bootstrap-order risk), plus channel send and the asyncio `Task`/`Future` result slots. It also doesn't reduce C++ much (`UninitArrayStorage` stays C++; you move one wrapper and gain a TPy `__move__`/bookkeeping body). Do it on its own branch, verifying Rc `ptr()`-stability + cell layout + the core-types bootstrap; likely needs `/tpy-add-feature` given it touches core primitives. Surfaced reviewing the Phase-2 shape.
- **[LOW]** Wrap the `make_unique<Adapter<P, ConcreteT>>(arg)` pattern into a `tpy::box_dyn<P>(arg)` runtime helper. Today every `Own[@dynamic-protocol]` call-arg wrap site (`_gen_dynamic_protocol_own_arg` in `tpyc/codegen_cpp/expressions.py:824-841`) emits the full `std::make_unique<::tpy::Adapter<P, std::remove_cvref_t<decltype(arg)>>>(arg)` spelling — readable enough but ~80 chars of boilerplate per site, especially noisy for async-def call args where `P` is `::tpystd::coro::Cancellable<...>`. A trivial header-only helper (`template<typename P, typename T> std::unique_ptr<P> box_dyn(T&& v) { return std::make_unique<Adapter<P, std::remove_cvref_t<T>>>(std::forward<T>(v)); }` next to `Adapter` in `runtime/cpp/include/tpy/dynamic.hpp`) lets codegen emit `::tpy::box_dyn<P>(arg)` instead. Purely cosmetic — same allocation, same template instantiation; one extra template-inline step the compiler folds away. Applies to *every* dyn-protocol wrap site (not just async), so the snapshot-regen diff would span all `@dynamic`-touching tests (`protocols/`, `async/`, plus a few in `inference/`, `readonly/`, etc.). Worth doing as a small standalone cleanup -- keep separate from any behavior-changing branch so the snapshot churn is isolated. Pairs with the structural-conformance branch (line 830) which can fold into the same helper. Surfaced during `feat-cancellable-async-typing` review.
- **[LOW]** Cut heap allocations on cancellable-API call sites by re-introducing a static-protocol cancellable shape for inline-holder consumers. After `feat-cancellable-async-typing` shipped, every `asyncio.run` / `create_task` / `wait_for` call emits `make_unique<Adapter<Cancellable<T>, ConcreteCoro>>(coro())` at the call site -- one heap alloc per call. The alloc count matches the pre-change `_box_coro` path (which boxed the same way, just hidden inside the stdlib helper), so the change is allocation-neutral; the optimization opportunity is reducing it. Two avoidable cases: (a) **`wait_for`-shaped inline holders** -- template `_WaitForFuture[T_aw, T]` over the concrete coro type (mirror of the `STATIC_PROTOCOL` `_CoroParamKind` machinery used for generic async methods today) so `_inner: T_aw` stores by value. Public signature stays `Own[Cancellable[T]]`; consumers become template-deduced. Trade-off: code size grows with the number of distinct coro types passed to `wait_for`. Implementation route: add a `STATIC_CANCELLABLE` shape paralleling `STATIC_PROTOCOL` but with a `Cancellable`-concept constraint (`__poll__` + `cancel`), or just make `Cancellable[T]` available in both `@dynamic` and non-`@dynamic` forms picked per consumer; (b) **`asyncio.run(main_coro)` main-task on stack** -- the main coro doesn't need the executor's slot table (which is for *spawned* tasks). Drive it via templated `poll_with_cancel` directly. ~30 lines of executor change, saves one alloc per `run()` call. The slot-table erasure for `create_task` is fundamental (heterogeneous container of `Box[AnyTask]`) and not in scope. Defer until allocation profiling actually surfaces this as a real hot path.

## Ownership & Consuming Iteration
- **`take()` builtin: move a value out of an Optional field, leaving `None` (Rust's `Option::take`).** Today a field read binds a borrow, so moving out of `x: T | None` (e.g. `conn = self._connection; self._connection = None; pool[k] = conn`) is rejected as a copy of a `@nocopy` type -- probe-verified during the keep-alive branch. `take(self.sock)` (or `self.sock.take()`) would move the payload out and backfill `None` atomically, the canonical idiom for owned-slot handoff. Known consumers waiting on it: `requests.Session._connection` seam -> pool routing, and the http.client will_close ownership-transfer redesign (below, Standard Library section) where `getresponse()` moves the transport into the response. Sibling consideration per feature discipline: the same shape applies to any owned-slot field (`Box | None`, `socket | None`, `SSLSocket | None`), and the design should say whether plain `T | None` unions and non-Optional owned fields (full-move + poison) are in or out of scope. Needs `/tpy-add-feature`.
- Consuming `items()` for dict: consuming `__iter__` works for keys, but consuming `items()` can't use `@overload` with `Own[Self]` because the return type changes (`dict_items` -> `Iterator[tuple]`), breaking non-iteration callers like `len(d.items())`. Needs a mechanism that only selects consuming dispatch when used as a for-loop iterable.
- `own_iter()` is list-only: kept as explicit escape hatch for non-last-use consuming. Could be made generic (dispatch to consuming `__iter__` on any type).
- `own()` builtin: explicit `T -> Own[T]` conversion (analogous to `span()` -> `Span[T]`).
- User-defined drain iterators (e.g. `ArrayListDrainIter`): view types with borrow tracking. See `docs/CONSUMING_ITERATION_DESIGN.md`.
- Codegen `std::move` for owned `self.field` returns: sema types `Own[Self].field` correctly as `Own[T]`, but codegen emits `return this->inner;` instead of `return std::move(this->inner);`. Breaks any `@nocopy` / unique-ownership field pattern with a C++ "use of deleted function X(const X&)" error from the copy path. Reproduces with `Own[Inner]` field + method body that mutates other state before `return self.inner`. Noted as "Partial" in `docs/CONSUMING_ITERATION_DESIGN.md` status table.
- [LOW, missed optimization] `borrow_reassigned_vars` is an add-only accumulator: a local that cycles owned -> borrow-alias -> owned-again stays barred from the movable set, so its final consume copies instead of moving. Safe (a copy is always sound for reference types), but pessimises that rare pattern. A precise fix would discard from the set on re-ownership, but that reintroduces the flow-sensitivity the union accumulator avoids -- defer until the THIR place/loan model makes per-binding movability natural.
- [LOW, missed inlining] The out-of-line ctor size partition (`records.py` `_gen_ctor_def`) counts the RAW `__init__` body, but field-init assigns hoist into the MIL -- a pure-field-init ctor with >7 `self.x = x` lines lands non-inline in the `.cpp` although its emitted def is a trivial `R::R(...) : a(a), ... {}` MIL one-liner, losing cross-TU inlining on a hot path. Count the post-hoist residue instead (needs the hoist decision earlier, or a cheap "simple self-field assign" pre-count). Surfaced by the /tpy-ready retrospective of the ctor out-of-line fix.
- [LOW, cleanup] `_store_analysis_results` (`sema/analyzer.py`) computes the exported movable set as a 3-clause exclusion cascade over `ever_owned_locals` (skip hoisted, skip reassigned+lvalue_reassigned, skip borrow_reassigned). Consolidate the exclusion sources into a single `never_movable_locals` set built incrementally as statements are analyzed, so the post-pass filter collapses to `ever_owned - never_movable` and a future exclusion reason is single-site. Aligns with the THIR direction (per-place movability); defer until then.

## Build pipeline
- **REPL latency track.** Per-eval cost today is ~0.8s entry-TU compile (g++ + PCH) + ~0.1s link; measured levers, in increasing value: (a) `-fuse-ld=mold` when detected (link 70ms -> 20ms on a small object set; grows with session object count); (b) ccache on the REPL's support-object compiles (kills the first-eval bulk cost across sessions -- generated TUs churn per eval and don't benefit); (c) the real fix: the clang-repl JIT backend (~20-80ms/expr, 10-40x) -- needs clang-repl >= 19 (std::expected, see the preflight-reject clang < 19 entry above) AND the managed-libs/runtime-impls gap closed by loading a prebuilt support `.so` into the interpreter (BUGS.md clang-repl entry). Compiler shopping is NOT a lever: clang++ is only ~12-14% faster than g++ at -O0 on the runtime-shaped workload (measured 2026-07-03), and PCH flattens most of that. Surfaced by the REPL-latency question after fix-repl-managed-libs.
- **[design] Managed-dep granularity: `datetime`'s tz backend is linked (and gated by `--date`) even for pure-calendar use.** `lib/tpy/datetime.py` imports `_bindings.hinnant_date` at module level, so `from datetime import timedelta` -- pure calendar math with zero OS dependency, which worked dep-free in v1 -- now links tz.cpp + date_shim.cpp into the binary, and `--date=none` turns ANY datetime import into a compile error. For `re`->pcre2 / `ssl`->mbedtls the dep is intrinsic to the whole module; datetime is the first managed lib where half the surface doesn't need the backend. Options: (a) document-and-accept (a note in DATETIME_DESIGN.md's backend section records the coupling); (b) link a managed dep only when one of its `@native` symbols is actually referenced by the compiled program (general dead-dep elimination -- pairs with the existing "do not compile .cpp files that are not needed" item below); (c) split the wall-clock surface into a submodule imported on use (fights CPython's single-module shape). Needs `/tpy-add-feature` if (b)/(c). Surfaced by the /tpy-ready second-opinion retrospective of datetime v2.
- **Stop writing the legacy `libtpy`/`runtime` session-fingerprint keys.** `tests/.session_fingerprints.json` still records both keys although (per CLAUDE.md) they no longer gate any phase -- every branch churns them on the first local run, producing recurring pure-noise commits and cross-branch merge conflicts (datetime-v2 alone carried two churn commits + one merge conflict on this file). Drop the writers (and the keys) in conftest; `cpy_stubs` stays. Surfaced by the /tpy-ready retrospective of datetime v2.
- **[nit] Rename the third-party `c_sources` plumbing to `third_party_sources`.** The field/params (`ThirdPartyBuildPlan.c_sources`, `build_cpp_commands(c_sources=...)`, conftest's `third_party_c_sources`) now carry `.cpp` files too (Hinnant date; driver picked per suffix by `third_party_source_driver`); comments are accurate but the names lie. Mechanical rename across compiler.py/cli.py/conftest.py. Surfaced by the /tpy-ready second-opinion retrospective of datetime v2.
- **Test harness: link only the imported stdlib `.o` per case (exec-cache precision).** Today every case links the WHOLE precompiled stdlib `.o` set (`precompiled_objects=cache.objects`) with no `--gc-sections`, so an unimported module is still in the binary. The exec fingerprint therefore folds in `stdlib_output_hash` (the full set's identity) to stay sound -- which means any stdlib-codegen change re-keys EVERY case. If instead a case linked only the `.o` for the modules it imports (its `all_modules` stdlib entries, which are the transitive import closure), the link-set would equal the hashed-set: the `stdlib_output_hash` term could drop, giving full per-case cache precision (a stdlib edit re-verifies only the cases that actually use the changed module). Risks to design through `/tpy-add-feature`: transitive C++ symbol deps that don't map 1:1 to TPy imports (under-link -> undefined-symbol link errors), and bundled C deps (PCRE2 `.o`) that are separate objects needing inclusion when their module is imported. Pure optimization (current design is sound); do it on its own branch. Surfaced by /tpy-ready on the exec-cache branch.
- **Test coverage: cross-module-cycle records with `__del__`/`__copy__` are unwitnessed.** The out-of-line special-member emission forces cycle members' dtor/move/copy defs into the `.cpp` (`records.py` def-mode `cycle_peers` gates); intra-module and size-driven partitions are covered (`records/recursive_record_mutual_{del,copy}`), but no case combines a cross-module record cycle with `__del__`/`__copy__`. Needs a two-module cycle fixture like `imports/mutual_cycle_record_reexport` plus special members. Surfaced by /tpy-review of the ctor out-of-line fix.
- **Test harness: no automated test for the exec-phase UX (`--no-exec`, the exec tally, the start-of-run cause line).** The soundness-critical `compute_exec_fingerprint` is unit-tested (`tests/test_exec_fingerprint.py`), but the plugin behaviors that sit on top of it are only manually verified: `--no-exec` skips exec while keeping cpy + its mutual-exclusion guard; the `tpy| exec: N built+run, M skipped` terminal tally (incl. xdist worker->controller aggregation via `workeroutput`); the `report_exec_env_change` cause line (fires on a shared-input change, suppressed under forced modes, per-checkout record). Cover with pytest's `pytester` fixture (in-process sub-pytest runs asserting on stdout) -- low priority; these are dev-tooling, not compiler behavior. Surfaced by /tpy-ready on the exec-cache branch.
- when a module forwards imports only, those imports should not generate headers if the symbols are not used
- do not compile .cpp files that are not needed, e.g. no function is called etc
- **(low prio) Route CLI builds through the shared PCH/stdlib cache.** `tpy file.py` builds up to three PCH variants (~160MB each: plain + `debug/` + `release/`) plus stdlib objects into every program's `__tpyc__/<prog>.d/`, so a hello-world build dir weighs ~480MB and a worktree's `examples/` ~3.5GB. The test harness already solved this -- shared, content-addressed PCH + stdlib `.o` in `~/.cache/tpyc` (see conftest's `get_stdlib_cache` / `get_pch_header`); the CLI path (`compiler.get_or_build_pch`, which stores the PCH "inside the build output directory") never adopted it. Re-plumb the CLI to the shared cache with the same invalidation keys (runtime-header hash, compiler identity, flags -- note the existing gap: the current staleness check compares only header mtimes, see BUGS-adjacent note in the PCH-cache entry of the audit). Per-program build dirs then shrink to generated sources + objects + binary. `/tpy-add-feature`-shaped: cache keying and concurrent-build locking need a design pass.
- **(low prio) Registry-drive the managed-third-party CLI wiring.** Adding a managed lib today is a coordinated three-file edit -- argparse flag registration in `cli.py`, the `third_party_modes` dict literal (`{"pcre2": args.pcre2, "mbedtls": args.mbedtls}`), and `_FACTORIES` in `third_party.py` -- yet only `_FACTORIES` is genuinely registry-driven. The flag name already lives on `ThirdPartyLib.cli_flag`, so the argparse registration loop and the modes dict could both be derived from `known_lib_names()` + `getattr(args, ...)`, leaving `_FACTORIES` as the single source of truth. Threshold now met: the third managed lib (`date`, datetime v2) landed with exactly this three-file edit -- pick up on the next build-layer touch. Surfaced by /tpy-review (architecture-fit) of the mbedTLS wiring.
- **(low prio) De-duplicate the vendor-script sidecar helpers.** `scripts/vendor_cacert.py`, `vendor_mbedtls.py`, and `vendor_pcre2.py` now carry a verbatim-identical `_stamp` / `_read_sidecar` / `_read_sidecar_version` / `_write_sidecar` shape (nothing lib-specific in the timestamp-reuse + sidecar-read logic). A shared `scripts/_vendor_common.py` with `stamp(sidecar_path, metadata)` / `read_sidecar(path)` would remove the triplication without disturbing the documented "one vendor script per lib" pattern. Threshold now met: the fourth vendor script (`vendor_date.py`, datetime v2) landed as another verbatim copy -- fold in on the next vendoring touch. Surfaced by /tpy-review (architecture-fit) of the bundled CA store.
- **Fill in package metadata before public release.** `pyproject.toml` declares only `name`/`description`/`Homepage = https://tpy.dev`. Add `authors`/`maintainers`, a `Repository` URL (deferred while the GitHub repo is private), and a `license` field + `LICENSE` file (project is currently unlicensed = all-rights-reserved; pick proprietary vs OSS when going public). Optionally `keywords` + trove `classifiers` for PyPI discoverability.

## Compiler Performance / mypyc

Goal: speed up test runs (currently ~3 min parallel, ~6 min single-threaded for non-slow tests) and prepare for self-hosting. These optimizations are worth doing independently but also remove mypyc blockers.

### Hotspot fixes (ordered by impact)

- Memoize type transformations: parked. PERF TODO note left at `substitute_type_params` in `sema/type_ops.py` for the next candidate; needs profiling data post-mypyc before acting.
- `isinstance` overhead on type predicates: `TypeKind` / `TpyType.tag` was retired when primitive subclasses collapsed to `NominalType` singletons; predicates now dispatch through `type_def_of(t).category` (dict lookup + attribute) or compound isinstance for structural types -- a per-call cost regression vs the prior frozenset tag lookups. Real win lands under mypyc; pre-mypyc profiling hasn't been done. Pre-mypyc mitigations noted as `PERF TODO` comments at the hot paths (`coercions.py::resolve_coercion`, `type_def_registry.py::type_def_of`, `typesys.py::is_numeric_type` family) -- hash-table coercion dispatch and per-instance category caching on `NominalType` are the two concrete knobs if a profile shows the dispatch is bottlenecking.

### mypy compliance (~1086 errors remaining)

- `TypeGuard` on predicate helpers (`is_protocol_type` -> `TypeGuard[NominalType]`, etc.) -- ~86 `attr-defined` errors
- `Scope | None` union-attr errors (67) -- genuinely optional, needs per-site narrowing
- `arg-type` (448), `assignment` (175) -- scattered, per-file mechanical work
- `object.__setattr__` in `PtrType.__post_init__` -- refactor to avoid

### mypyc integration (after mypy compliance)

- switch from hatchling to setuptools with mypyc build step (or mypycify)
- keep macro loader as pure Python, exclude from mypyc compilation

## Investigate
- Unify the two inherited-subst composition copies into one shared helper (e.g. `type_ops.compose_type_substs`): `tpyc/sema/methods.py` `_analyze_instance_method` composes inherited x instance substs one level (with an `isinstance(v, TpyType)` guard for `N: int` bindings), while `tpyc/sema/protocols.py:740-758` has a more complete twin that also transitively resolves `TypeParamRef` chains. Hand-converged duplicates drift. While unifying, verify multi-hop generic-through-generic chains with a test (`class Mid[V](Bag[V, 8]); class Leaf[W](Mid[W])` -- the flat merge in `lookup_record_method_overloads` plus one-level composition may leave the declaring param bound to the intermediate one; unverified, surfaced by /tpy-review of the subclass-receiver bound fix).
- Polymorphic integer literals (Rust-style). Today `IntLiteralType` is resolved at the assignment site to `default_int_type` (Int32 by default), so `a = 0; f(a)` for `f(x: UInt64)` requires either `a: UInt64 = 0` or a cast. The current `literal_default_vars` retro-widening covers function-local cases that re-fit a fixed-int target on first typed-slot use, but module-level constants (`CONST = 5`), collection-element literals (`weights = [10, 20, 30]; send_byte(weights[0])`), and locals reassigned from a non-literal expression don't benefit. The full alternative would be to keep `IntLiteralType` polymorphic *everywhere* until first use that demands a concrete type -- the consistent mental model is "literals adapt to context anywhere", but the cost surface is wide: comparisons, prints, `for x in xs:` iter element types, generic inference, container element-type unification, and ~all sema sites that read `var_types[id(decl)]` mid-flow would need to handle "still IntLit" or trigger resolution. Also requires a deliberate generic-inference policy (do unbound `T` resolve to default_int from a literal arg, or look ahead at constraints?). Defer until the retro-widen cliff (function-local mutation drops the seed; conflicting demands lock to first usage) starts producing user complaints in real code.
- CPython native (C) module for tpy stubs: the `lib/cpy/tpy/` stubs are pure Python. A C extension module could improve CPython performance for programs that use tpy types (Int32, Array, Span, etc.) heavily.
- investigate rust like feature (borrowing, lifetimes etc) to make the language safe; however these should be softer restrictions than in rust
- zig language: what it is, how is it different from C, what useful patterns can we learn
- Distinct types (newtypes): `class Meters(Distinct[float]): pass` -- zero-cost wrapper that creates a nominally distinct type. Prevents mixing incompatible values of the same underlying type (units, IDs, currencies). C++ codegen: thin struct wrapper or strong typedef. Inspired by Nim's `distinct` keyword.
- `@constexpr` decorator: mark pure functions as compile-time evaluable, emit C++ `constexpr`. Useful for lookup tables, constants, config. Low implementation cost -- lean on C++ compiler. `TABLE: Final[Int32] = factorial(10)` computed at compile time.
- Inline iterators: `@inline` on generator functions to unroll the loop body at the call site instead of creating a state machine. Zero overhead, important for hot paths. Generalizes what `range()` already does implicitly to user-defined iterators. Inspired by Nim's inline vs closure iterator distinction.
- Runtime exception migration -- see `docs/EXCEPTION_DESIGN.md` for the per-type migration table (status, sites, divergences). Pending items live there, not here.
- Better runtime panic message for unhandled `@error_return` calls. Currently `_maybe_error_return_unwrap` (`tpyc/codegen_cpp/expressions.py`) emits `tpy_panic("unhandled error return")` which is opaque -- doesn't include the function name or the error value's data. Improve to `tpy_panic("unhandled X in <func>: " + e.message)` or similar. Affects all return-tier panic sites uniformly.

## Builtins
- **`min(iterable)` / `max(iterable)` single-iterable form.** Only the variadic `min(a, b, ...)` form exists today; `min(xs)` over a `list`/iterable -- the *primary* Python usage -- errors with "No matching overload for min(list[Int32])". Add the iterable-reducing overload (and `key=`/`default=` to follow). Common first-non-trivial-program idiom. Surfaced by /tpy-fuzzy-test (collections), 0.3.0 smoke test.
- **`reverse=` keyword on `list.sort()` and `sorted()`.** Both reject `reverse=True` ("does not support keyword argument 'reverse'"). Descending sort is a first-page Python idiom; today it needs a custom comparator. Add the `reverse: bool = False` keyword to both. Surfaced by /tpy-fuzzy-test (collections), 0.3.0 smoke test.
- **Builtins as first-class function values.** `key=len`, `map(str, xs)`, etc. fail with "'len' is not a variable" -- builtin names can't be passed as callables (only user `def`s can). The diagnostic should at least suggest wrapping (`def my_len(s: str) -> Int32: return len(s)`); the feature is making builtin references materialize as callable values. Surfaced by /tpy-fuzzy-test (collections), 0.3.0 smoke test.
- Generic Fn param type deduction: `sorted[T,K](Iterable[T], Fn[[T],K])` shares T between iterable and Fn, so the Fn param can widen T (e.g. `sorted([3,1,4], key=negate)` widens T from Int32 to BigInt). In C++, T and KeyFn are independent template params -- the key function's type is deduced separately and implicit conversion handles the call. The proper TPy fix: support independent callable type params bounded over other params, e.g. `sorted[T, KFn: Callable[[T], Comparable]](Iterable[T], KFn)`. Current behavior (widening) is correct but suboptimal; explicit `list[Int32]` + BigInt key already errors.
- type(); (in future `T = type(x); z = T()`)
- tpy.ctypes.CInt32
- ptr() function? Auto-select Ptr vs Ptr[readonly[T]] based on binding mutability. Needs sema-level magic (mutability not in type, it's in binding context).
- `tpy.unsafe.unsafe_address_of(x) -> int`: return the memory address of an object as an integer. Useful for identity comparison in tests (proving reference semantics vs silent copy). C++ codegen: `reinterpret_cast<uintptr_t>(&x)`.

## Python features
- **Multi-exception `except (A, B):` tuple syntax.** Rejected today with a clean diagnostic ("'except' requires a simple or dotted name"). The single-class and multiple-`except`-clause forms work, so the workaround is one `except` clause per type (each with its own handler body). The tuple form is extremely common Python (catching a set of related errors with one handler) -- e.g. `except (IsADirectoryError, PermissionError)` for an OS op whose errno class differs per platform. Codegen would lower one `catch` block testing membership in the tuple's types. Surfaced fixing the macOS `os_mutate_errors` test (the remove-on-a-dir errno divergence).
- **Nested comprehensions** (`[x for row in grid for x in row]`). Rejected today with a clean "Nested comprehensions not yet supported"; the double-`for` form is common enough (flattening, cartesian products) to schedule. Surfaced by /tpy-fuzzy-test (collections), 0.3.0 smoke test.
- **`object` and `type` as annotation types.** `object` errors with "Unknown type: object"; `type` with "'type' cannot be used as a type annotation".
